People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Well… I suppose I’m here because I’ve gotten into a bit of a habit—maybe more than a bit—of checking on things at home. I keep opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.

I know that probably sounds silly, because sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but I find myself going back again anyway. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.

Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.

I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or little labeled boxes to make sure the papers and keepsakes are still there, even if I only checked a few minutes earlier.\n\nI know that sounds unnecessary, and I’m a bit embarrassed saying it, but I suppose that’s the thing that keeps pulling me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.

I keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I’ve gotten into a bit of a habit—maybe more than a bit—of checking on things at home. I keep opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know that probably sounds silly, because sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but I find myself going back again anyway. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check on things because you're worried something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not exactly something terrible, I don’t think… at least I hope not. It’s more that I feel very unsettled unless I look again. I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or little labeled boxes to make sure the papers and keepsakes are still there, even if I only checked a few minutes earlier.\n\nI know that sounds unnecessary, and I’m a bit embarrassed saying it, but I suppose that’s the thing that keeps pulling me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I keep opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know that probably sounds silly, because sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but I find myself going back again anyway. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check on things because you're worried something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not exactly something terrible, I don’t think… at least I hope not. It’s more that I feel very unsettled unless I look again. I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or little labeled boxes to make sure the papers and keepsakes are still there, even if I only checked a few minutes earlier.\n\nI know that sounds unnecessary, and I’m a bit embarrassed saying it, but I suppose that’s the thing that keeps pulling me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check on things because you're worried something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not exactly something terrible, I don’t think… at least I hope not. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or little labeled boxes to make sure the papers and keepsakes are still there, even if I only checked a few minutes earlier.\n\nI know that sounds unnecessary, and I’m a bit embarrassed saying it, but I suppose that’s the thing that keeps pulling me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. If it were someone reliable, I’m sure that would help some.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I’ve gotten into a bit of a habit—maybe more than a bit—of checking on things at home. I keep opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know that probably sounds silly, because sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but I find myself going back again anyway. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check on things because you're worried something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not exactly something terrible, I don’t think… at least I hope not. It’s more that I feel very unsettled unless I look again. I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or little labeled boxes to make sure the papers and keepsakes are still there, even if I only checked a few minutes earlier.\n\nI know that sounds unnecessary, and I’m a bit embarrassed saying it, but I suppose that’s the thing that keeps pulling me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check on these things if someone you trusted was responsible for keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. If it were someone reliable, I’m sure that would help some.\n\nBut I know myself—I’d probably still feel the urge to open the folder or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes, just to see with my own eyes that the papers and keepsakes were still there. Even if they’d only just told me, or I’d checked a few minutes before. It sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s the part that’s been taking up so much of my day, and why I thought I should ask for help.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it’s more about feeling in control of my surroundings, if that makes sense. Not control in a grand way, I don’t mean that… but just needing to know that the important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I’ve gotten into a bit of a habit—maybe more than a bit—of checking on things at home. I keep opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know that probably sounds silly, because sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but I find myself going back again anyway. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check on things because you're worried something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not exactly something terrible, I don’t think… at least I hope not. It’s more that I feel very unsettled unless I look again. I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or little labeled boxes to make sure the papers and keepsakes are still there, even if I only checked a few minutes earlier.\n\nI know that sounds unnecessary, and I’m a bit embarrassed saying it, but I suppose that’s the thing that keeps pulling me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check on these things if someone you trusted was responsible for keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. Even if they’d only just told me, or I’d checked a few minutes before. It sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s the part that’s been taking up so much of my day, and why I thought I should ask for help.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling in control of my surroundings, if that makes sense. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check on these things if someone you trusted was responsible for keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. If it were someone reliable, I’m sure that would help some.\n\nBut I know myself—I’d probably still feel the urge to open the folder or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes, just to see with my own eyes that the papers and keepsakes were still there. Even if they’d only just told me, or I’d checked a few minutes before. It sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s the part that’s been taking up so much of my day, and why I thought I should ask for help.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling in control of my surroundings, if that makes sense. Not control in a grand way, I don’t mean that… but just needing to know that the important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I spend far too much of the day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, even when I only checked a few minutes before. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, but it feels hard to settle unless I’ve looked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I’ve gotten into a bit of a habit—maybe more than a bit—of checking on things at home. I keep opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know that probably sounds silly, because sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but I find myself going back again anyway. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check on things because you're worried something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not exactly something terrible, I don’t think… at least I hope not. It’s more that I feel very unsettled unless I look again. I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or little labeled boxes to make sure the papers and keepsakes are still there, even if I only checked a few minutes earlier.\n\nI know that sounds unnecessary, and I’m a bit embarrassed saying it, but I suppose that’s the thing that keeps pulling me back to check.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check on these things if someone you trusted was responsible for keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. Even if they’d only just told me, or I’d checked a few minutes before. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, but it feels hard to settle unless I’ve looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious if you didn't have to check on these things so often?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check on these things if someone you trusted was responsible for keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. If it were someone reliable, I’m sure that would help some.\n\nBut I know myself—I’d probably still feel the urge to open the folder or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes, just to see with my own eyes that the papers and keepsakes were still there. Even if they’d only just told me, or I’d checked a few minutes before. It sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s the part that’s been taking up so much of my day, and why I thought I should ask for help.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling in control of my surroundings, if that makes sense. Not control in a grand way, I don’t mean that… but just needing to know that the important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I spend far too much of the day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, even when I only checked a few minutes before. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, but it feels hard to settle unless I’ve looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious if you didn't have to check on these things so often?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it would be a relief not to feel pulled back to the same folders or drawers all the time.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’m spending far too much of my day opening the same things—folders, labeled boxes, drawers—just to be sure the papers and little keepsakes are where I left them, even if I checked only a few minutes earlier. If I didn’t feel I had to do that so often, I do think I’d feel calmer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because I’ve gotten into a bit of a habit—maybe more than a bit—of checking on things at home. I keep opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes to make sure my important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know that probably sounds silly, because sometimes I’ve only just checked a few minutes before, but I find myself going back again anyway. It’s taking up more of my day than I’d like, and I thought maybe I should ask someone about it… if that’s all right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever check on things because you're worried something bad might happen if you don't?\n\nPatient: Not exactly something terrible, I don’t think… at least I hope not. It’s more that I feel very unsettled unless I look again. It’s usually a little thought that keeps needling at me, like maybe I threw away my husband’s old tax papers by mistake, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nEven when I know, really, that I probably didn’t, I can’t quite let the thought pass. It feels as if I’ve been terribly careless, and then it just sits there in my mind. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check on these things if someone you trusted was responsible for keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. If it were someone reliable, I’m sure that would help some.\n\nBut I know myself—I’d probably still feel the urge to open the folder or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes, just to see with my own eyes that the papers and keepsakes were still there. Even if they’d only just told me, or I’d checked a few minutes before. It sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s the part that’s been taking up so much of my day, and why I thought I should ask for help.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling in control of my surroundings, if that makes sense. Not control in a grand way, I don’t mean that… but just needing to know that the important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I spend far too much of the day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, even when I only checked a few minutes before. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, but it feels hard to settle unless I’ve looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious if you didn't have to check on these things so often?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it would be a relief not to feel pulled back to the same folders or drawers all the time.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’m spending far too much of my day opening the same things—folders, labeled boxes, drawers—just to be sure the papers and little keepsakes are where I left them, even if I checked only a few minutes earlier. If I didn’t feel I had to do that so often, I do think I’d feel calmer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is more about preventing mistakes or making sure everything is in its rightful place?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more… making sure everything is in its rightful place. Or at least where I believe I left it.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but that’s really what brought me in. I spend too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes, just checking that the important papers and keepsakes are still there—even if I looked only a few minutes before. It’s not so much that I’m trying to prevent some big mistake, I suppose… it’s more that I can’t quite relax unless I see they’re where they should be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do these little thoughts that keep needling at you feel like they're coming from outside your own thinking, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, I think. Not like a voice or anything from outside me. It’s more… even when I know better, the thought keeps poking at me that perhaps I threw away my husband’s old tax papers, or put a family recipe in the wrong box.\n\nI know that may sound a bit ordinary, but I can’t seem to just let it pass. I start feeling as though I’ve done something terribly careless, even when part of me knows I probably haven’t.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking on these things to prevent something specific from happening?\n\nPatient: Not to prevent something specific, exactly. It’s more that I get this uneasy feeling and I think, “I should just make sure.” So I’ll open the same folders, drawers, or those little labeled boxes again, just to see the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them.\n\nI know I may have checked only a few minutes before, which sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud… but I can lose quite a lot of time doing it. I suppose that’s why I came in, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you avoid making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it does, in a way. It makes me feel as though I’m being careful… that I haven’t misplaced something important or made some foolish mistake.\n\nBut I know I go too far with it. That’s really why I came, because I spend far too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, just to make sure the papers and keepsakes are where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes before. It’s embarrassing to admit, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to check things a specific number of times, or in a specific order, to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not a particular number, no… at least I don’t think so. It isn’t like I have to do it three times or in a special order.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel settled until I’ve looked again. I’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those little labeled boxes, just to reassure myself the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which I know sounds a bit much. But I can spend far too much of the day doing it, I’m afraid.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a specific place or arranged in a certain way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t know if I’d call it “arranged” in a fussy way. It’s more that I want things to be where they belong, in the folders or drawers or those labeled boxes.\n\nI keep opening them to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them. Even if I only checked a few minutes ago. I know that may sound excessive, but I can spend far too much of the day doing it, and that’s really what brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or perfectly arranged for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s symmetry, exactly. I’m not especially bothered if something isn’t perfectly lined up or arranged just so.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed that being the issue. It’s more… well, I suppose I just like things to be in their proper place, but not in a perfectly neat or matching sort of way, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking on these things helps you feel more in control of your life or your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, in a way. Or at least it feels like it might, for a little while.\n\nWhen I open the same folders or drawers or those labeled boxes and see the papers and keepsakes are still there, where I left them, I feel a bit reassured. I know I may have only checked a few minutes before, so it isn’t terribly sensible, but it gives me a moment of feeling that things are all right. That’s why I came, really—because I’m spending far too much of the day doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the act of checking on these things helps you maintain a sense of order in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not that I’m trying to make everything perfect, exactly, but opening the same folders and drawers and the little labeled boxes does make me feel like things are still in order.\n\nI know I’ve often just checked them a few minutes before, which sounds a bit foolish, I suppose. But I keep wanting to make sure the important papers and keepsakes are still where I left them. And I’m afraid I’ve been spending far too much of my day doing that—that’s why I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would stop if you felt completely confident in your ability to keep track of things?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I suppose if I felt entirely sure of myself, maybe it would help… but even when I know, really, that I’ve kept track of things, I still find myself going back.\n\nI’ll open the same folders or drawers, or those labeled boxes, just to make sure the important papers and little keepsakes are still where I left them. Sometimes I’ve only checked a few minutes before, which is a bit embarrassing to admit. I don’t mean to make such a fuss over it, but it does take up far too much of my day—that’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check on these things if someone you trusted was responsible for keeping track of them?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe I’d still want to look, to be honest. Not because I wouldn’t trust the person, necessarily. If it were someone reliable, I’m sure that would help some.\n\nBut I know myself—I’d probably still feel the urge to open the folder or the drawer, or one of those labeled boxes, just to see with my own eyes that the papers and keepsakes were still there. Even if they’d only just told me, or I’d checked a few minutes before. It sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, but that’s the part that’s been taking up so much of my day, and why I thought I should ask for help.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling in control of my surroundings, if that makes sense. Not control in a grand way, I don’t mean that… but just needing to know that the important papers and keepsakes are exactly where I left them.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I spend far too much of the day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes again and again, even when I only checked a few minutes before. I know it probably sounds unnecessary, but it feels hard to settle unless I’ve looked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious if you didn't have to check on these things so often?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it would be a relief not to feel pulled back to the same folders or drawers all the time.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’m spending far too much of my day opening the same things—folders, labeled boxes, drawers—just to be sure the papers and little keepsakes are where I left them, even if I checked only a few minutes earlier. I spend too much of my day opening the same folders, drawers, and labeled boxes, just checking that the important papers and keepsakes are still there—even if I looked only a few minutes before. It’s not so much that I’m trying to prevent some big mistake, I suppose… it’s more that I can’t quite relax unless I see they’re where they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking would decrease if you felt more confident that everything was in its proper place?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it probably would decrease… if I could really trust that everything was where it belonged.\n\nI know I’ve said this already, and I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, but that’s the part that’s been taking up so much of my day.
